# Randomized quasipolynomial-time mean-payoff games

```json
{
  "abstract_verbatim": "We give a randomized algorithm that computes the complete zero-threshold winning set of a finite mean-payoff game with arbitrary signed integer edge weights encoded in binary. For total explicit input length <i>L</i>, it uses $`2^{O((\\log(L+2))^2)}`$ bit operations on every random tape and is correct with probability at least 7/8. A polynomial-time check certifies the winning regions and positional strategies for both players or reports failure. Independent repetition therefore gives an always-correct algorithm with the same expected quasipolynomial bit bound.",
```

<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We give a randomized algorithm that computes the complete zero-threshold winning set of a finite mean-payoff game with arbitrary signed integer edge weights encoded in binary. For total explicit input length <i>L</i>, it uses $`2^{O((\log(L+2))^2)}`$ bit operations on every random tape and is correct with probability at least 7/8. A polynomial-time check certifies the winning regions and positional strategies for both players or reports failure. Independent repetition therefore gives an always-correct algorithm with the same expected quasipolynomial bit bound.

- **claim-main-randomized-algorithm** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is a uniform randomized algorithm that, for every such game, returns the entire set $$U=\{v\in V:\text{there is a Max strategy }\sigma \text{ such that for every Min strategy }\tau,\ \operatorname{MP}_w(\pi_{v,\sigma,\tau})\ge0\}$$ correctly with probability at least $7/8$. For an absolute constant $C>0$, on every random tape its total cost is at most $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$$ bit operations in a standard model polynomially equivalent to a uniform probabilistic Turing machine. The bound includes preprocessing, recursive calls, intermediate arithmetic, and random-bit generation.
  - Explanation: A uniform randomized algorithm returns the complete zero-threshold winning set of a mean-payoff game with probability at least 7/8. Its bit cost on every random tape is quasipolynomial in the input length.
- **claim-cycle-signs** (theorem, [source](#stmt-box-cycle-signs)): Every simple directed cycle has nonzero $t$-sum. Its $t$-sum is positive exactly when its $w$-sum is nonnegative, and negative exactly when its $w$-sum is negative.
  - Explanation: The integer perturbation of the weights keeps the sign of every simple cycle sum and never produces a zero sum.
- **claim-box-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-box-comparison)): Let $F$ be the transformed operator in Equation <a href="#eq:operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operator">[eq:operator]</a>. For arbitrary integer bounds $a\le b$, every subsolution $y\in[a,b]$ and supersolution $z\in[a,b]$ satisfy $y_i\le z_i$ for *all* $i\in V$. This includes boxes with negative, nonconstant, or collapsed coordinate intervals.
  - Explanation: In any finite integer box, every subsolution lies coordinatewise below every supersolution, including boxes with negative, varying or collapsed intervals.
- **claim-box-solution** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-box-solution)): For the same operator $F$ and any finite integer box $[a,b]$, there is exactly one vector $x\in[a,b]$ that is both a subsolution and a supersolution. It is exactly the unique fixed point of $$T_{a,b}(p)=\mathop{\mathrm{clip}}_{[a,b]}F(p).$$ The synchronous procedure $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Basic}}(a,b)$ initializes $p=a$, evaluates $q=T_{a,b}(p)$, returns $p$ if $q=p$, and otherwise replaces $p$ by $q$ and repeats. It returns $x$ after at most $$1+\sum_{i\in V}(b_i-a_i)\le1+nB$$ operator evaluations, including the final stability test, for any reference width $B$. No positivity or nondegeneracy of the coordinate intervals is assumed.
  - Explanation: Each finite integer box has exactly one vector that is both a subsolution and a supersolution. It is the fixed point of the clipped operator, and the basic iteration reaches it within the stated number of evaluations.
- **claim-game-reduction** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-game-reduction)): Let $x$ solve the constant box $[0,B_*]$, with $B_*=8nW$ as in Equation <a href="#eq:transformed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:transformed">[eq:transformed]</a>. Then the original game’s exact zero-threshold winning set is $$U=\{i\in V:x_i>B_*/2\}.$$ A positional Max strategy guarantees original mean payoff at least zero from every vertex of $U$. Outside $U$, a positional Min strategy ensures that every consistent play has original upper limiting average at most $-1/n$, including against arbitrary history-dependent Max strategies.
  - Explanation: Thresholding the solution of the wide constant box at its midpoint gives the winning set. Positional strategies for both players follow from the two potential bands.
- **claim-small-rank** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-small-rank)): If $R<1$, the vector $$p_i=\begin{cases}b_i,&\beta_i>m,\\a_i,&\beta_i\le m\end{cases}$$ satisfies $y\le p\le z$ for every admissible subsolution $y$ and every admissible supersolution $z$ of $[a,b]$.
  - Explanation: When the rank parameter is below one, an explicit vector separates every admissible subsolution from every admissible supersolution, so no recursion is needed.
- **claim-reweighting** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-reweighting)): Let $H\subseteq V$ satisfy $\mu(H)\le1/2$, and keep the box $[a,b]$ fixed.* 1. *Set $\alpha'_i=\alpha_i/2$ on $H$ and $\alpha'_i=\alpha_i$ off $H$, and set $k'=3k/5$, leaving $\beta,m$ unchanged. Every admissible subsolution $y$ with $\alpha(S_y\cap H)>\eta k$ remains admissible. Every formerly admissible supersolution remains admissible as well.* 2. *Set $\beta'_i=\beta_i/2$ on $H$ and $\beta'_i=\beta_i$ off $H$, and set $m'=3m/5$, leaving $\alpha,k$ unchanged. Every admissible supersolution $z$ with $\beta(S_z\cap H)>\eta m$ remains admissible, as does every formerly admissible subsolution.* *In either case, $$R'=\frac35(1+\mu(H))R\le\frac9{10}R.$$
  - Explanation: Halving the weights on a set of small measure and shrinking the budget keeps the relevant comparisons admissible while lowering the rank parameter by a fixed factor.
- **claim-boundary-projections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-boundary-projections)): Let $y$ be a subsolution and $z$ a supersolution of $[a,b]$.* 1. *If $a\le a'\le b$, then $y'=\max(y,a')$ is a subsolution of $[a',b]$ with $S_{y'}=\{i:y_i>a'_i\}\subseteq S_y$.* 2. *If $a\le b'\le b$, then $z'=\min(z,b')$ is a supersolution of $[a,b']$ with $S_{z'}=\{i:z_i<b'_i\}\subseteq S_z$.* *These projections preserve admissibility for unchanged weights and budgets. Lowering only the upper bound also preserves any subsolution that still fits, with unchanged support; raising only the lower bound preserves any supersolution that still fits, with unchanged support. The new bounds need not satisfy an inequality involving $F$.
  - Explanation: Moving a box boundary and projecting a comparison onto the new box gives a comparison of the new box whose support can only shrink, so admissibility is preserved.
- **claim-center-projections** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-center-projections)): Let $q\in[a,b]$ be an integer vector and $A$ a positive integer.* 1. *For a subsolution $y$ of $[a,b]$, if $d=\max_i(y_i-q_i)\ge2A$, then $$y'=\max(y-d+A,q-A)$$ is a subsolution of $[q-A,q+A]$ with support $$S_{y'}=\{i:y_i-q_i>d-2A\}\subseteq S_y.$$* 2. *For a supersolution $z$ of $[a,b]$, if $e=\max_i(q_i-z_i)\ge2A$, then $$z'=\min(z+e-A,q+A)$$ is a supersolution of $[q-A,q+A]$ with support $$S_{z'}=\{i:q_i-z_i>e-2A\}\subseteq S_z.$$* *Each projected comparison is admissible when the parent’s comparison is admissible and the support weights and budget are unchanged.
  - Explanation: A comparison can be translated and clipped into a box centered at a given vector. The result is a comparison there with exactly described support contained in the parent support.
- **claim-tightening-projection** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-tightening-projection)): Suppose $B>128s$ and define $$\delta=\floor{B/(4s)},\qquad C=B-\delta,\qquad h=1/s.$$ Then $\delta>0$, and the following statements hold for $[a,b]$.* 1. *If $y$ is a subsolution with $d=\max_i(y_i-a_i)>C$, then $u=\max(y-d+\delta,a)$ is a subsolution in the same box, $u\le y$, and $$\begin{aligned} S_u&=\{i:y_i-a_i>d-\delta\} \subseteq\{i:y_i\ge a_i+B-hB\},\\ \max_i(u_i-a_i)&=\delta. \end{aligned}$$* 2. *If $z$ is a supersolution with $e=\max_i(b_i-z_i)>C$, then $v=\min(z+e-\delta,b)$ is a supersolution in the same box, $v\ge z$, and $$\begin{aligned} S_v&=\{i:b_i-z_i>e-\delta\} \subseteq\{i:z_i\le b_i-B+hB\},\\ \max_i(b_i-v_i)&=\delta. \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: When a comparison reaches far toward a boundary of a wide box, a shifted copy is again a comparison with support confined to the extreme layer and with the prescribed maximal displacement.
- **claim-termination-bounds** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-termination)): For every permitted $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$ input, the execution and all its subroutine executions terminate on every random tape. The outputs of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$ and $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Ask}}$ are integer vectors in their respective input boxes. The outputs of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Orient}}$ and $\ensuremath{\mathsf{OrientPlus}}$ have the stated choice and list types, and each listed set has input measure at most $1/2$. Moreover:* 1. *Tightening makes at most $8ns$ updates and $16ns+2$ calls to $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Ask}}$.* 2. *A chain of main $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$ continuations contains at most $$\label{eq:main-chain-length} K=8s(g+1)+1$$ solver invocations.* 3. *One $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Orient}}$ trial visits at most $g+1$ orientation invocations and returns at most $2g+2$ sets. Consequently $\ensuremath{\mathsf{OrientPlus}}$ returns at most $J(2g+2)$ sets, counting repetitions.
  - Explanation: Every execution of the recursive procedures terminates on every random tape with outputs of the right type. The number of tightening updates, the main chain length and the number of orientation sets are bounded.
- **claim-orientation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-orientation)): Assume the lower-rank comparison guarantees stated at the beginning of this section. On a fixed permitted input $(a,b;B,\alpha,\beta,k,m)$ of rank $r$, let $(\chi,\mathcal H)$ be the output of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Orient}}$. For every fixed admissible subsolution $y$ of $[a,b]$ whose core in Equation <a href="#eq:orientation-cores" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:orientation-cores">[eq:orientation-cores]</a> satisfies $\alpha(I_y)>\eta k$, $$\label{ori:positive-alternative} \Pr\left( \chi=Y \ \text{or}\ \exists H\in\mathcal H: \alpha(S_y\cap H)>\eta k \right)\geq 1-\frac1{16}.$$ Separately, for every fixed admissible supersolution $z$ of $[a,b]$ with $\beta(I_z)>\eta m$, $$\label{ori:negative-alternative} \Pr\left( \chi=Z \ \text{or}\ \exists H\in\mathcal H: \beta(S_z\cap H)>\eta m \right)\geq 1-\frac1{16}.$$ On every tape, every set in $\mathcal H$ has $\mu$-measure at most $1/2$. Each probability statement remains valid conditional on a pre-call history that fixes its input and qualifying comparison, when the call uses fresh random bits.
  - Explanation: For each fixed qualifying comparison, one orientation call either chooses the safe side or returns a set carrying enough support weight, with high probability. All returned sets have small measure on every tape.
- **claim-ask-amplification** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-ask-amplification)): Let $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Ask}}$ have an input of rank less than $r$, and let $p$ be its returned vector. For each fixed admissible subsolution $y$ of its input box, and separately for each fixed admissible supersolution $z$, $$\Pr(p\not\geq y)\leq\varepsilon, \qquad \Pr(p\not\leq z)\leq\varepsilon.$$ Both conclusions also hold conditional on any pre-call history that determines the input and the indicated admissible comparison, provided the call uses fresh randomness.
  - Explanation: The coordinatewise median of repeated solver calls dominates a fixed admissible subsolution, or is dominated by a fixed supersolution, except with very small probability.
- **claim-center-estimates** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ori-center-estimates)): Let $B>128s$, and let $q_0,\ldots,q_s$ be the centers in Step <a href="#alg:orient-loop" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="alg:orient-loop">[alg:orient-loop]</a> of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Orient}}$. Put $$D=\left\lfloor\frac{B}{32s}\right\rfloor, \qquad E_0=\left\lfloor\frac B8\right\rfloor.$$ Then $q_j\in[a,b]$ and $\lvert q_{j+1,i}-q_{j,i}\rvert\leq D$ for every applicable $i,j$, on every random tape. Moreover, $$\label{ori:radius-estimates} D\geq\frac{B}{64s}>0, \qquad sD\leq E_0, \qquad 2D\leq h(2E_0).$$ For a subsolution $y$ with $I_y\ne\varnothing$, set $d_j=\max_i(y_i-q_{j,i})$. Then $$\begin{aligned} d_j&\geq B/2-hB-sD-1\geq 2E_0 &&(0\leq j\leq s), \label{ori:positive-deviation}\\ d_0-(y_i-q_{0,i})&\leq hB+1 &&(i\in I_y). \label{ori:positive-deficit} \end{aligned}$$ For a supersolution $z$ with $I_z\ne\varnothing$, set $e_j=\max_i(q_{j,i}-z_i)$. Then $$\begin{aligned} e_j&\geq B/2-hB-sD-1\geq 2E_0 &&(0\leq j\leq s), \label{ori:negative-deviation}\\ e_0-(q_{0,i}-z_i)&\leq hB+1 &&(i\in I_z). \label{ori:negative-deficit} \end{aligned}$$ The coordinate widths may be unequal and may be zero.
  - Explanation: The centers of the orientation loop stay in the box and move little per step. The deviation of a fixed comparison from them is bounded below, with controlled initial deficits on the extreme core.
- **claim-deviation-drops** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ori-deviation-drops)): For each fixed admissible subsolution $y$ with nonempty high core, with probability at least $1-2s\varepsilon$ every loop step satisfies $$d_{j+1}\leq d_j, \qquad d_{j+1}\leq d_j-D\quad\hbox{at every light time for \(y\)}.$$ Separately, for each fixed admissible supersolution $z$ with nonempty low core, with probability at least $1-2s\varepsilon$ every step satisfies $$e_{j+1}\leq e_j, \qquad e_{j+1}\leq e_j-D\quad\hbox{at every light time for \(z\)}.$$
  - Explanation: With high probability the maximal deviation of a fixed comparison never increases along the loop and drops by a fixed amount at every light time.
- **claim-core-displacement** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ori-core-displacement)): On the subsolution event of Lemma <a href="#ori:deviation-drops" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ori:deviation-drops">21</a>, $I_y\cap N=\varnothing$; if there are more than $256$ light times for $y$, then $I_y\subseteq P$. On the separate supersolution event, $I_z\cap P=\varnothing$; more than $256$ light times for $z$ imply $I_z\subseteq N$.
  - Explanation: On the deviation event the extreme core avoids the opposite displacement set, and many light times push the whole core into the matching displacement set.
- **claim-orient-plus** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-orient-plus)): Under the lower-rank assumption of this section, the output of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{OrientPlus}}$ satisfies Equation <a href="#ori:positive-alternative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ori:positive-alternative">[ori:positive-alternative]</a> for each fixed qualifying subsolution and, separately, Equation <a href="#ori:negative-alternative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ori:negative-alternative">[ori:negative-alternative]</a> for each fixed qualifying supersolution, with failure probability at most $\varepsilon$ in each case. The same bounds hold conditional on a history that fixes the input and comparison before the call’s fresh randomness. On every tape its list has length at most $J(2g+2)$, and each listed set has measure at most $1/2$.
  - Explanation: Repeating orientation with a majority vote on the choice and concatenation of all lists amplifies the orientation alternative, while keeping the list length and measure bounds on every tape.
- **claim-conditioning** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sol-conditioning)): Suppose a randomized procedure has failure probability at most $\rho$ for every fixed input and every fixed comparison satisfying a specified qualification condition. Let $\mathcal H$ be the history before a call. Assume that its input, proposed comparison, and qualification are determined by $\mathcal H$, and that the call uses fresh randomness. Then $$\Pr\bigl(\text{qualification holds and the comparison fails} \mid\mathcal H\bigr) \le \rho\,\mathbf 1_{\{\text{qualification holds}\}}.$$ The same bound may be used after restricting to any event determined by this pre-call history.
  - Explanation: A pointwise failure bound still holds when the input and the comparison are determined by earlier random choices, provided the call uses fresh randomness.
- **claim-tightening-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sol-tightening)): Assume the comparison guarantees at every smaller rank. Consider a $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$ tightening loop with $B>128s$, and fix an admissible subsolution $y^{\mathrm{in}}$ before the loop begins. Outside an event of probability at most $(16ns+2)\varepsilon$, its final box $[a,b]$ contains an admissible subsolution $y\ge y^{\mathrm{in}}$, obtained by successive lower-bound projections, such that $$\max_i(y_i-a_i)>C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \alpha\bigl(\{i:y_i\ge a_i+B-hB\}\bigr)>\eta k, \qquad C=B-\delta. \label{sol:tightened-high-core}$$ Separately, for each admissible supersolution $z^{\mathrm{in}}$ fixed before the loop, outside an event with the same probability bound, the final box contains an admissible $z\le z^{\mathrm{in}}$ satisfying $$\max_i(b_i-z_i)>C \quad\Longrightarrow\quad \beta\bigl(\{i:z_i\le b_i-B+hB\}\bigr)>\eta m. \label{sol:tightened-low-core}$$ Both assertions also hold conditionally on any preceding history that determines the loop input and the initial comparison.
  - Explanation: Through the tightening loop a fixed comparison is transported to the final box, and if it threatens the later truncation its extreme core is heavy. The failure probability is bounded by the number of requests times the amplified error.
- **claim-solve-comparisons** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-solve-comparisons)): Let $[a,b]$ be an allowed integer box with positive integer reference width $B\le B_*$, and let the support weights and budgets be positive rationals. For the output $p$ of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$, each fixed admissible subsolution $y$ and, separately, each fixed admissible supersolution $z$ satisfy $$\Pr(p\ge y)\ge 1-\frac1{16}, \qquad \Pr(p\le z)\ge 1-\frac1{16}.$$ These are whole-vector inequalities, as in Equation <a href="#eq:comparison-guarantees" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-guarantees">[eq:comparison-guarantees]</a>: a fixed comparison is chosen before the invocation, and its inequality holds at every coordinate on its success event.
  - Explanation: The solver output dominates each fixed admissible subsolution and is dominated by each fixed admissible supersolution with high probability, proved by induction on the rank along the main chain.
- **claim-top-success** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-top-success)): Let $x$ be the box solution for $[0,B_*]$. Let $p$ be the output of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$ on that box with unit support weights, budgets $k=m=n$, and reference width $B_*$. Then $$\Pr(p=x)\ge\frac78.$$ Hence $\{i:p_i>B_*/2\}$ is the complete winning set with probability at least $7/8$.
  - Explanation: Applying both pointwise guarantees to the exact top-box solution recovers it entirely with probability at least 7/8, which gives the complete winning set.
- **claim-bit-complexity** (bound, [source](#stmt-thm-bit-complexity)): The algorithm has a uniform implementation in a standard bit-operation model such that, for every input of bit length $L$ and every random tape, the running time is at most $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$$ for an absolute constant $C>0$. The bound includes preprocessing, every recursive execution, intermediate arithmetic, and random-bit generation.
  - Explanation: The total bit cost, including preprocessing, recursion, arithmetic and random bits, is quasipolynomial in the input length on every random tape.
- **claim-certified-output** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-certified-output)): There is a randomized algorithm which, on every tape, uses $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$ bit operations and returns either a failure symbol or the exact winning set together with an integer potential and positional strategies for both players. Whenever it returns these objects, the Max strategy wins from every vertex of the winning set and the Min strategy ensures upper limiting average at most $-1/n$ from every other vertex, against arbitrary opposing strategies. It returns these objects with probability at least $7/8$.* *Repeating independent trials until a certificate is accepted terminates almost surely, never returns an incorrect answer, and has expected bit cost $2^{O((\log_2(L+2))^2)}$.
  - Explanation: A polynomial-time check of the returned potential certifies the winning sets and positional strategies or reports failure. Repetition until acceptance gives an always-correct algorithm with the same expected bound.

It is exactly the unique fixed point of $$T_{a,b}(p)=\\mathop{\\mathrm{clip}}_{[a,b]}F(p).$$ The synchronous procedure $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Basic}}(a,b)$ initializes $p=a$, evaluates $q=T_{a,b}(p)$, returns $p$ if $q=p$, and otherwise replaces $p$ by $q$ and repeats. It returns $x$ after at most $$1+\\sum_{i\\in V}(b_i-a_i)\\le1+nB$$ operator evaluations, including the final stability test, for any reference width $B$. No positivity or nondegeneracy of the coordinate intervals is assumed.",
```json
          "source_id": "PM-104.4"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For the same operator $F$ and any finite integer box $[a,b]$, there is exactly one vector $x\\in[a,b]$ that is both a subsolution and a supersolution. It returns $x$ after at most $$1+\\sum_{i\\in V}(b_i-a_i)\\le1+nB$$ operator evaluations, including the final stability test, for any reference width $B$.         "anchor": "stmt-prop-game-reduction",
        "exact_quote": "Let $x$ solve the constant box $[0,B_*]$, with $B_*=8nW$ as in Equation <a href=\"#eq:transformed\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:transformed\">[eq:transformed]</a>. Then the original game’s exact zero-threshold winning set is $$U=\\{i\\in V:x_i>B_*/2\\}.$$ A positional Max strategy guarantees original mean payoff at least zero from every vertex of $U$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-reweighting",
        "exact_quote": "Let $H\\subseteq V$ satisfy $\\mu(H)\\le1/2$, and keep the box $[a,b]$ fixed.*\n\n1.  *Set $\\alpha'_i=\\alpha_i/2$ on $H$ and $\\alpha'_i=\\alpha_i$ off $H$, and set $k'=3k/5$, leaving $\\beta,m$ unchanged. Every admissible subsolution $y$ with $\\alpha(S_y\\cap H)>\\eta k$ remains admissible. Every formerly admissible supersolution remains admissible as well.*\n\n2.  *Set $\\beta'_i=\\beta_i/2$ on $H$ and $\\beta'_i=\\beta_i$ off $H$, and set $m'=3m/5$, leaving $\\alpha,k$ unchanged.         "source_id": "PM-104.4"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-boundary-projections",
        "exact_quote": "Let $y$ be a subsolution and $z$ a supersolution of $[a,b]$.*\n\n1.  *If $a\\le a'\\le b$, then $y'=\\max(y,a')$ is a subsolution of $[a',b]$ with $S_{y'}=\\{i:y_i>a'_i\\}\\subseteq S_y$.*\n\n2.  *If $a\\le b'\\le b$, then $z'=\\min(z,b')$ is a supersolution of $[a,b']$ with $S_{z'}=\\{i:z_i<b'_i\\}\\subseteq S_z$.*\n\n*These projections preserve admissibility for unchanged weights and budgets. Lowering only the upper bound also preserves any subsolution that still fits, with unchanged support; raising only the lower bound preserves any supersolution that still fits, with unchanged support. The new bounds need not satisfy an inequality involving $F$.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-center-projections",
        "exact_quote": "Let $q\\in[a,b]$ be an integer vector and $A$ a positive integer.*\n\n1.  *For a subsolution $y$ of $[a,b]$, if $d=\\max_i(y_i-q_i)\\ge2A$, then $$y'=\\max(y-d+A,q-A)$$ is a subsolution of $[q-A,q+A]$ with support $$S_{y'}=\\{i:y_i-q_i>d-2A\\}\\subseteq S_y.$$*\n\n2.  *For a supersolution $z$ of $[a,b]$, if $e=\\max_i(q_i-z_i)\\ge2A$, then $$z'=\\min(z+e-A,q+A)$$ is a supersolution of $[q-A,q+A]$ with support $$S_{z'}=\\{i:q_i-z_i>e-2A\\}\\subseteq S_z.$$*\n\n*Each projected comparison is admissible when the parent’s comparison is admissible and the support weights and budget are unchanged.",
        "source_id": "PM-104.4"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-104.4"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Let $q\\in[a,b]$ be an integer vector and $A$ a positive integer.*\n\n1.  *For a subsolution $y$ of $[a,b]$, if $d=\\max_i(y_i-q_i)\\ge2A$, then $$y'=\\max(y-d+A,q-A)$$ is a subsolution of $[q-A,q+A]$ with support $$S_{y'}=\\{i:y_i-q_i>d-2A\\}\\subseteq S_y.$$*\n\n2.  *For a supersolution $z$ of $[a,b]$, if $e=\\max_i(q_i-z_i)\\ge2A$, then $$z'=\\min(z+e-A,q+A)$$ is a supersolution of $[q-A,q+A]$ with support $$S_{z'}=\\{i:q_i-z_i>e-2A\\}\\subseteq S_z.$$*\n\n*Each projected comparison is admissible when the parent’s comparison is admissible and the support weights and budget are unchanged.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-tightening-projection",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $B>128s$ and define $$\\delta=\\floor{B/(4s)},\\qquad C=B-\\delta,\\qquad h=1/s.$$ Then $\\delta>0$, and the following statements hold for $[a,b]$.*\n\n1.  *If $y$ is a subsolution with $d=\\max_i(y_i-a_i)>C$, then $u=\\max(y-d+\\delta,a)$ is a subsolution in the same box, $u\\le y$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n     S_u&=\\{i:y_i-a_i>d-\\delta\\}\n           \\subseteq\\{i:y_i\\ge a_i+B-hB\\},\\\\\n     \\max_i(u_i-a_i)&=\\delta.\n    \\end{aligned}$$*\n\n2.  *If $z$ is a supersolution with $e=\\max_i(b_i-z_i)>C$, then $v=\\min(z+e-\\delta,b)$ is a supersolution in the same box, $v\\ge z$, and $$\\begin{aligned}\n     S_v&=\\{i:b_i-z_i>e-\\delta\\}\n           \\subseteq\\{i:z_i\\le b_i-B+hB\\},\\\\\n     \\max_i(b_i-v_i)&=\\delta.\n    \\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-104.4"
      },
      "evidence": [

          "source_id": "PM-104.4"

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-termination",
        "exact_quote": "For every permitted $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Solve}}$ input, the execution and all its subroutine executions terminate on every random tape. The outputs of $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Solve}}$ and $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Ask}}$ are integer vectors in their respective input boxes. The outputs of $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Orient}}$ and $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{OrientPlus}}$ have the stated choice and list types, and each listed set has input measure at most $1/2$. Moreover:*\n\n1.  *Tightening makes at most $8ns$ updates and $16ns+2$ calls to $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Ask}}$.*\n\n\n2.  *A chain of main $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Solve}}$ continuations contains at most $$\\label{eq:main-chain-length}\n     K=8s(g+1)+1$$ solver invocations.*\n\n3.  *One $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Orient}}$ trial visits at most $g+1$ orientation invocations and returns at most $2g+2$ sets. Consequently $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{OrientPlus}}$ returns at most $J(2g+2)$ sets, counting repetitions.",
        "source_id": "PM-104.4"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-orientation",
        "exact_quote": "Assume the lower-rank comparison guarantees stated at the beginning of this section. On a fixed permitted input $(a,b;B,\\alpha,\\beta,k,m)$ of rank $r$, let $(\\chi,\\mathcal H)$ be the output of $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Orient}}$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-ask-amplification",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Ask}}$ have an input of rank less than $r$, and let $p$ be its returned vector.         "exact_quote": "Let $B>128s$, and let $q_0,\\ldots,q_s$ be the centers in Step <a href=\"#alg:orient-loop\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"alg:orient-loop\">[alg:orient-loop]</a> of $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Orient}}$. Put $$D=\\left\\lfloor\\frac{B}{32s}\\right\\rfloor,\n  \\qquad\n  E_0=\\left\\lfloor\\frac B8\\right\\rfloor.$$ Then $q_j\\in[a,b]$ and $\\lvert q_{j+1,i}-q_{j,i}\\rvert\\leq D$ for every applicable $i,j$, on every random tape. Moreover, $$\\label{ori:radius-estimates}\n  D\\geq\\frac{B}{64s}>0,\n  \\qquad\n  sD\\leq E_0,\n  \\qquad\n  2D\\leq h(2E_0).$$ For a subsolution $y$ with $I_y\\ne\\varnothing$, set $d_j=\\max_i(y_i-q_{j,i})$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n  d_j&\\geq B/2-hB-sD-1\\geq 2E_0\n       &&(0\\leq j\\leq s), \\label{ori:positive-deviation}\\\\\n  d_0-(y_i-q_{0,i})&\\leq hB+1\n       &&(i\\in I_y). \\label{ori:positive-deficit}\n\\end{aligned}$$ For a supersolution $z$ with $I_z\\ne\\varnothing$, set $e_j=\\max_i(q_{j,i}-z_i)$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n  e_j&\\geq B/2-hB-sD-1\\geq 2E_0\n       &&(0\\leq j\\leq s), \\label{ori:negative-deviation}\\\\\n  e_0-(q_{0,i}-z_i)&\\leq hB+1\n       &&(i\\in I_z). \\label{ori:negative-deficit}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The coordinate widths may be unequal and may be zero.",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ori-deviation-drops",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sol-conditioning",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose a randomized procedure has failure probability at most $\\rho$ for every fixed input and every fixed comparison satisfying a specified qualification condition. Let $\\mathcal H$ be the history before a call. Assume that its input, proposed comparison, and qualification are determined by $\\mathcal H$, and that the call uses fresh randomness. Then $$\\Pr\\bigl(\\text{qualification holds and the comparison fails}\n              \\mid\\mathcal H\\bigr)\n \\le \\rho\\,\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\text{qualification holds}\\}}.$$ The same bound may be used after restricting to any event determined by this pre-call history.",
        "source_id": "PM-104.4"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-sol-conditioning",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose a randomized procedure has failure probability at most $\\rho$ for every fixed input and every fixed comparison satisfying a specified qualification condition. Let $\\mathcal H$ be the history before a call. Assume that its input, proposed comparison, and qualification are determined by $\\mathcal H$, and that the call uses fresh randomness. Then $$\\Pr\\bigl(\\text{qualification holds and the comparison fails}\n              \\mid\\mathcal H\\bigr)\n \\le \\rho\\,\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\text{qualification holds}\\}}.$$ The same bound may be used after restricting to any event determined by this pre-call history.",
          "source_id": "PM-104.4"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Suppose a randomized procedure has failure probability at most $\\rho$ for every fixed input and every fixed comparison satisfying a specified qualification condition. Let $\\mathcal H$ be the history before a call. Assume that its input, proposed comparison, and qualification are determined by $\\mathcal H$, and that the call uses fresh randomness. Then $$\\Pr\\bigl(\\text{qualification holds and the comparison fails}\n              \\mid\\mathcal H\\bigr)\n \\le \\rho\\,\\mathbf 1_{\\{\\text{qualification holds}\\}}.$$ The same bound may be used after restricting to any event determined by this pre-call history.",
      "explanation": "A pointwise failure bound still holds when the input and the comparison are determined by earlier random choices, provided the call uses fresh randomness.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-sol-conditioning",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose a randomized procedure has failure probability at most $\\rho$ for every fixed input and every fixed comparison satisfying a specified qualification condition.",
          "source_id": "PM-104.4"

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sol-tightening",
        "exact_quote": "Assume the comparison guarantees at every smaller rank. Consider a $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Solve}}$ tightening loop with $B>128s$, and fix an admissible subsolution $y^{\\mathrm{in}}$ before the loop begins. Outside an event of probability at most $(16ns+2)\\varepsilon$, its final box $[a,b]$ contains an admissible subsolution $y\\ge y^{\\mathrm{in}}$, obtained by successive lower-bound projections, such that $$\\max_i(y_i-a_i)>C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\alpha\\bigl(\\{i:y_i\\ge a_i+B-hB\\}\\bigr)>\\eta k,\n \\qquad C=B-\\delta.\n \\label{sol:tightened-high-core}$$ Separately, for each admissible supersolution $z^{\\mathrm{in}}$ fixed before the loop, outside an event with the same probability bound, the final box contains an admissible $z\\le z^{\\mathrm{in}}$ satisfying $$\\max_i(b_i-z_i)>C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\beta\\bigl(\\{i:z_i\\le b_i-B+hB\\}\\bigr)>\\eta m.\n \\label{sol:tightened-low-core}$$ Both assertions also hold conditionally on any preceding history that determines the loop input and the initial comparison.",
        "source_id": "PM-104.4"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-sol-tightening",
          "exact_quote": "Assume the comparison guarantees at every smaller rank. Consider a $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Solve}}$ tightening loop with $B>128s$, and fix an admissible subsolution $y^{\\mathrm{in}}$ before the loop begins. Outside an event of probability at most $(16ns+2)\\varepsilon$, its final box $[a,b]$ contains an admissible subsolution $y\\ge y^{\\mathrm{in}}$, obtained by successive lower-bound projections, such that $$\\max_i(y_i-a_i)>C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\alpha\\bigl(\\{i:y_i\\ge a_i+B-hB\\}\\bigr)>\\eta k,\n \\qquad C=B-\\delta.\n \\label{sol:tightened-high-core}$$ Separately, for each admissible supersolution $z^{\\mathrm{in}}$ fixed before the loop, outside an event with the same probability bound, the final box contains an admissible $z\\le z^{\\mathrm{in}}$ satisfying $$\\max_i(b_i-z_i)>C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\beta\\bigl(\\{i:z_i\\le b_i-B+hB\\}\\bigr)>\\eta m.\n \\label{sol:tightened-low-core}$$ Both assertions also hold conditionally on any preceding history that determines the loop input and the initial comparison.",
          "source_id": "PM-104.4"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Assume the comparison guarantees at every smaller rank. Consider a $\\ensuremath{\\mathsf{Solve}}$ tightening loop with $B>128s$, and fix an admissible subsolution $y^{\\mathrm{in}}$ before the loop begins. Outside an event of probability at most $(16ns+2)\\varepsilon$, its final box $[a,b]$ contains an admissible subsolution $y\\ge y^{\\mathrm{in}}$, obtained by successive lower-bound projections, such that $$\\max_i(y_i-a_i)>C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\alpha\\bigl(\\{i:y_i\\ge a_i+B-hB\\}\\bigr)>\\eta k,\n \\qquad C=B-\\delta.\n \\label{sol:tightened-high-core}$$ Separately, for each admissible supersolution $z^{\\mathrm{in}}$ fixed before the loop, outside an event with the same probability bound, the final box contains an admissible $z\\le z^{\\mathrm{in}}$ satisfying $$\\max_i(b_i-z_i)>C\n \\quad\\Longrightarrow\\quad\n \\beta\\bigl(\\{i:z_i\\le b_i-B+hB\\}\\bigr)>\\eta m.\n \\label{sol:tightened-low-core}$$ Both assertions also hold conditionally on any preceding history that determines the loop input and the initial comparison.",
```

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Box comparisons (subsolution and supersolution)** ([source](#stmt-def-box-comparisons)): For a finite integer box $[a,b]$, a *subsolution* is a full vector $y\in[a,b]$ satisfying $$y_i\le F_i(y)\quad(i\in S_y),\qquad S_y=\{i\in V:y_i>a_i\}.$$ A *supersolution* is a full vector $z\in[a,b]$ satisfying $$z_i\ge F_i(z)\quad(i\in S_z),\qquad S_z=\{i\in V:z_i<b_i\}.$$ We call $S_y$ and $S_z$ their *supports*, always relative to the specified box. At a collapsed coordinate $a_i=b_i$, neither support contains $i$ and neither inequality is required.
- **Admissible comparisons** ([source](#stmt-def-admissible-comparison)): A subsolution $y$ of $[a,b]$ is *admissible* if $\alpha(S_y)\le k$. A supersolution $z$ of that box is admissible if $\beta(S_z)\le m$. The supports are relative to $[a,b]$ and have the boundary conventions of Definition <a href="#def:box-comparisons" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:box-comparisons">3</a>.
- **Reference width** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A positive integer $B$ is a *reference width* if $b_i-a_i\le B$ for all $i$.
- **Repeated comparison Ask** ([source](#stmt-proc-ask)): For its given solver input, $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Ask}}$ runs $J$ independent copies of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Solve}}$ and returns their coordinatewise median.
- **Repeated orientation OrientPlus** ([source](#stmt-proc-orient-plus)): For its given orientation input, $\ensuremath{\mathsf{OrientPlus}}$ runs $J$ independent copies of $\ensuremath{\mathsf{Orient}}$. It returns the majority choice in $\{Y,Z\}$ and the concatenation of all exceptional-set lists in trial order. It retains minority-trial lists and duplicate entries.

| q-top-recovery | Probability of exact top-box recovery | ≥ | $\frac78$ | Unit weights and root budgets |
| q-expected-trials | Expected number of certifying trials | ≤ | $8/7$ | Independent trials |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-cycle-signs** [claim](#stmt-box-cycle-signs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-box-cycle-signs): Direct computation of the perturbed cycle sum from cycle length and original sum. -> sign separation of simple cycles
- **step-box-comparison** [claim](#stmt-lem-box-comparison) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-box-comparison): Maximal difference argument with tight edges leading to a simple zero-sum cycle. -> subsolutions lie below supersolutions
- **step-box-solution** [claim](#stmt-lem-box-solution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-box-solution): Fixed-point characterization, monotone iteration from the lower bound, uniqueness by comparison. -> unique box solution and iteration count
- **step-game-reduction** [claim](#stmt-prop-game-reduction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-game-reduction): Two potential bands, tight-edge strategies, cycle decomposition of play prefixes. -> winning set by thresholding
- **step-small-rank** [claim](#stmt-lem-small-rank) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-small-rank): Coordinatewise case split on the weights. -> explicit separating vector
- **step-reweighting** [claim](#stmt-lem-reweighting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-reweighting): Support weight computation and reciprocal-product sum calculation. -> rank contraction
- **step-boundary-projections** [claim](#stmt-lem-boundary-projections) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-boundary-projections): Monotonicity of the operator applied to projected vectors. -> projected comparisons with shrinking support
- **step-center-projections** [claim](#stmt-lem-center-projections) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-center-projections): Translation and monotonicity of the operator. -> centered projections with exact supports
- **step-tightening-projection** [claim](#stmt-lem-tightening-projection) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-tightening-projection): Shift by delta and check the active coordinates. -> shifted comparisons with extreme-layer support
- **step-termination** [claim](#stmt-prop-termination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-termination): Induction on rank and on width, counting updates and chain length. -> termination and output types on every tape
- **step-ask-amplification** [claim](#stmt-lem-ask-amplification) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-ask-amplification): Median over independent trials with a union bound over failing subsets. -> small whole-vector failure probability
- **step-center-estimates** [claim](#stmt-ori-center-estimates) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ori-center-estimates): Floor and clipping arithmetic on the loop centers. -> radius and deviation estimates
- **step-deviation-drops** [claim](#stmt-ori-deviation-drops) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ori-deviation-drops): Conditional amplified comparisons on the projected comparison at each step. -> deviation monotone with drops at light times
- **step-core-displacement** [claim](#stmt-ori-core-displacement) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ori-core-displacement): Combine accumulated drops with initial deficits. -> core inside a displacement set
- **step-orientation** [claim](#stmt-prop-orientation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ori-core-displacement-2): Width induction: early returns by motion, otherwise sampled iterate and recursive transfer. -> orientation alternative
- **step-orient-plus** [claim](#stmt-lem-orient-plus) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-orient-plus): Majority vote with subset counting over independent trials. -> amplified orientation
- **step-conditioning** [claim](#stmt-sol-conditioning) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sol-conditioning): Condition on the pre-call history and average. -> pointwise bounds usable after earlier randomness
- **step-tightening-comparison** [claim](#stmt-sol-tightening) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sol-tightening): Protected comparison requests per call, first-error union bound. -> transported comparison with heavy extreme core
- **step-solve-comparisons** [claim](#stmt-thm-solve-comparisons) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-solve-comparisons): Induction on rank following one comparison along the main chain. -> pointwise comparison guarantees
- **step-top-success** [claim](#stmt-cor-top-success) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-top-success): Intersect the two whole-vector events for the exact top solution. -> exact recovery of the potential
- **step-bit-complexity** [claim](#stmt-thm-bit-complexity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-bit-complexity): Contract the main chain, bound magnitudes, exact rational representation, polynomial local work. -> quasipolynomial bit bound
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-bit-complexity-2): Run the solver on the top box and threshold the result. -> randomized winning-set algorithm
- **step-certified-output** [claim](#stmt-cor-certified-output) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-certified-output): Check the fixed-point equation, extract strategies from attaining edges, repeat until acceptance. -> certified and Las Vegas versions

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-cycle-sign-separation**: After the integer perturbation, every simple directed cycle has a nonzero transformed sum. Its transformed sum is positive exactly when its original sum is nonnegative. The reference code computes the perturbed weights and the box bound from this transformation.
- **inv-unique-box-solution**: The clipped operator iteration started at the lower corner of the box reaches the unique vector that is both a subsolution and a supersolution. The reference code runs exactly this iteration on the constant top box.
- **inv-threshold-winning-set**: The set of vertices whose coordinate of the top-box solution exceeds half the box height is the exact zero-threshold winning set of the original game.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-randomized-single-run**: Treat the output of one run of the randomized recursive solver as certainly correct. -> The randomized solver is only guaranteed correct with bounded probability, and its time bound holds even when the output is wrong. The deterministic box iteration in the reference code carries no such guarantee of speed.
- **anti-large-weights-iteration**: Use the plain box iteration on inputs whose binary-encoded weights are huge. -> The number of iterations is proportional to the box width, which is exponential in the binary length of the weights.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The full randomized algorithm is not reproduced. The deterministic finite core is: perturb the weights, run the clipped operator iteration on the top box, and threshold at half height. This computes the exact winning set and is checked against an independent brute-force oracle on small games.

```python
# INPUTS: stdin tokens: n m owners edges; owners is a string of n characters, 1 for Max and 0 for Min; then m triples u v w with 0-indexed vertices and integer weight.
# PRECONDITIONS: n >= 1, every vertex has an outgoing edge, weights are small integers.
# OUTPUTS: one line: the letter U followed by the sorted winning vertices, each preceded by a space.
# TIME: at most about n times the box width evaluations of the operator, each scanning all edges.
# SPACE: linear in n plus m.
# ARITHMETIC: exact Python integers.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and a nonzero exit.
import sys
def main():
    d = sys.stdin.read().split()
    n = int(d[0]); m = int(d[1]); own = d[2]
    E = [(int(d[3+3*k]), int(d[4+3*k]), int(d[5+3*k])) for k in range(m)]
    t = [(n+1)*w+1 for (u, v, w) in E]
    W = max([1] + [abs(x) for x in t])
    B = 8*n*W
    out = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for k, (u, v, w) in enumerate(E):
        out[u].append((v, t[k]))
    p = [0]*n
    while True:
        q = []
        for i in range(n):
            vals = [tt+p[j] for (j, tt) in out[i]]
            f = max if own[i] == '1' else min
            q.append(min(B, max(0, f(vals))))
        if q == p:
            break
        p = q
    U = [i for i in range(n) if 2*p[i] > B]
    print("U" + "".join(" " + str(i) for i in U))
main()
```

| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is a bit-complexity theorem for a recursive randomized solver, not a library or API contract. Any software use would need the full recursion with its exact rational parameters. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The game is an abstract infinite-duration arena used for decision, not a simulation environment. No simulation step is constructed. |

| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The paper gives an explicit randomized recursion (Solve, Ask, Orient, OrientPlus) and a certifying Las Vegas wrapper that tests whether the returned vector is a fixed point of the clipped operator. The wrapper is the directly implementable construction. The inner recursion is adaptable only with exact rational parameters. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Only a concrete representation is discussed for the proof, an incidence vector for vertex subsets. No new data structure with its own guarantee is given. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The cost model is a classical uniform machine model. No parallel or hardware-level design appears, and the exponent bound hides large constants. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No cryptographic primitive, hardness assumption or security notion is analyzed. The random bits serve only for algorithmic sampling. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The method is an exact combinatorial recursion, not statistical learning. The input is arbitrary and fixed and only the algorithm uses randomness. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The unique fixed point of a clipped min-max operator is characterized, but no optimisation model or solver contract is stated beyond the two-player winning set. Value computation is delegated to a companion article. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The game is a centralized sequential turn-based arena. No messages, faults or concurrency are modelled. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The appendix on a tropical substitution rule is a version-specific counterexample, not a reusable construction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-no-sinks** (hypothesis): The game model requires every vertex to have an outgoing edge, so plays are infinite. The result and its algorithm are stated only for such arenas. Blocked use: ['Running the solver on arenas that contain sink or terminating vertices without first adding an outgoing edge to each vertex.']
- **limit-integer-weights** (hypothesis): Edge weights are signed integers in binary. The cycle perturbation and the top-box height both rely on integrality. Blocked use: ['Feeding real-valued, floating-point or irrational weights to the perturbation and box construction.']
- **limit-two-player-ownership** (hypothesis): The arena is split between exactly two owners, Max and Min. The operator, the two potential bands and the strategy extraction use this partition. Blocked use: ['Applying the two-band thresholding to games with more than two players or without a strictly opposing partition of vertices.']
- **limit-single-run-error** (approximation): A single run of the randomized algorithm returns the right set only with bounded probability. The time bound holds even when the output is wrong. Blocked use: ['Treating the output of one unchecked run as certainly correct in a setting that tolerates no error.', 'Using the output as a verified certificate without the fixed-point check.']
- **limit-quasipolynomial-only** (formal_scope): The proven cost is quasipolynomial in the input length, not polynomial. Blocked use: ['Relying on a polynomial worst-case deadline for large binary-encoded inputs.']
- **limit-pointwise-guarantee** (formal_scope): The comparison guarantees of the solver are stated for one fixed admissible comparison at a time. They are not simultaneous over all comparisons. Blocked use: ['Using the guarantee as an event that holds at once for every admissible subsolution or supersolution in one run.']
- **limit-basic-iteration-width** (edge_case): The plain box iteration has a cost that grows with the reference width, which can be huge for binary-encoded endpoints. The recursive solver uses it only at small widths. Blocked use: ['Applying the basic iteration directly to wide boxes built from large weights.']
- **limit-orientation-rank** (hypothesis): Orientation is invoked only when the rank parameter is at least one. Below that threshold the solver returns an explicit vector directly. Blocked use: ['Calling the orientation procedure on inputs whose rank parameter is below one and expecting its alternative guarantee.']
- **limit-certified-repetition** (formal_scope): The repeated certifying procedure has a bound only in expectation. Each individual trial keeps the bound on every tape. Blocked use: ['Quoting the Las Vegas version as having a worst-case time bound over all tapes.']
- **limit-tropical-appendix** (counterexample_scope): The appendix counterexample concerns one printed substitution rule in one cited version. It says nothing about repaired rules or other versions. Blocked use: ['Citing the appendix as a refutation of other tropical optimization procedures or of a different version.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 104 (`lean/docs/104.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Deterministic quasipolynomial-time mean-payoff games](../../preprints/Deterministic-quasipolynomial-time-mean-payoff-games-September-25-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Randomized quasipolynomial-time mean-payoff games](../../preprints/Randomized-quasipolynomial-time-mean-payoff-games-September-25-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization checks that a specified two-step execution of Truffet's elimination procedure terminates with a feasible but nonoptimal output. It is a finite counterexample to that proposed optimization step.
>
> The formalized result gives one randomized algorithm for the complete zero-threshold winning set of every finite mean-payoff game with signed binary weights. It succeeds with probability at least $7/8$ and runs in quasipolynomial bit time on every random tape. Self-loops, parallel edges, and history-dependent strategies are allowed, and winning means a nonnegative liminf average payoff.

Comparator source `RandomizedMeanPayoff.lean` (4104 bytes, sha256 `0814324496e79fc843198a37954c4615543e7c6fdc09796f9f177c071a6439a5`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace MeanPayoff
structure Game where
  n : ℕ
  positive : 0 < n
  m : ℕ
  tail : Fin m → Fin n
  head : Fin m → Fin n
  maxOwner : Fin n → Bool
  weight : Fin m → ℤ
  total : ∀ v : Fin n, ∃ e : Fin m, tail e = v

def Strategy (G : Game) :=
  List (Fin G.m) → (v : Fin G.n) → {e : Fin G.m // G.tail e = v}

def move (G : Game) (σ τ : Strategy G) (v : Fin G.n)
    (history : List (Fin G.m)) : Fin G.m :=
  if G.maxOwner v then (σ history v).val else (τ history v).val

def position (G : Game) (σ τ : Strategy G) (start : Fin G.n) :
    ℕ → Fin G.n × List (Fin G.m)
  | 0 => (start, [])
  | t + 1 =>
    let p := position G σ τ start t
    let e := move G σ τ p.1 p.2
    (G.head e, p.2 ++ [e])

def play (G : Game) (σ τ : Strategy G) (start : Fin G.n) (t : ℕ) : Fin G.m :=
  let p := position G σ τ start t
  move G σ τ p.1 p.2

noncomputable def meanPayoff (G : Game) (π : ℕ → Fin G.m) : ℝ :=
  Filter.liminf (fun T : ℕ =>
    (∑ j ∈ Finset.range T, (G.weight (π j) : ℝ)) / (T : ℝ)) Filter.atTop

def Winning (G : Game) (v : Fin G.n) : Prop :=
  ∃ σ : Strategy G, ∀ τ : Strategy G, 0 ≤ meanPayoff G (play G σ τ v)

def encodeNat (n : ℕ) : List Bool :=
  let bits := Nat.bits (n + 1)
  List.replicate bits.length true ++ [false] ++ bits

def encodeInt (z : ℤ) : List Bool :=
  decide (z < 0) :: encodeNat z.natAbs

def input (G : Game) : List Bool :=
  encodeNat G.n ++ encodeNat G.m ++ List.ofFn G.maxOwner ++
    (List.ofFn (fun e : Fin G.m =>
      encodeNat ((G.tail e).val + 1) ++ encodeNat ((G.head e).val + 1) ++
        encodeInt (G.weight e))).flatten

abbrev Symbol := Option Bool

inductive Direction where
  | left | stay | right
  deriving DecidableEq

def Direction.offset : Direction → ℤ
  | .left => -1
  | .stay => 0
  | .right => 1

structure Action (q : ℕ) where
  next : Fin q
  write : Fin 3 → Symbol
  shift : Fin 3 → Direction

structure Machine where
  states : ℕ
  start : Fin states
  transition : Fin states → (Fin 3 → Symbol) → Bool → Option (Action states)

structure Configuration (M : Machine) where
  control : Option (Fin M.states)
  head : Fin 3 → ℤ
  tape : Fin 3 → ℤ → Symbol

def initial (M : Machine) (code : List Bool) : Configuration M where
  control := some M.start
  head := fun _ => 0
  tape := fun k j => if k = 0 ∧ 0 ≤ j then code[j.toNat]? else none

def step (M : Machine) (c : Configuration M) (bit : Bool) : Configuration M :=
  match c.control with
  | none => c
  | some s =>
    match M.transition s (fun k => c.tape k (c.head k)) bit with
    | none => { c with control := none }
    | some a =>
      { control := some a.next
        head := fun k => c.head k + (a.shift k).offset
        tape := fun k => Function.update (c.tape k) (c.head k) (a.write k) }

def run (M : Machine) (code : List Bool) (random : ℕ → Bool) : ℕ → Configuration M
  | 0 => initial M code
  | t + 1 => step M (run M code random t) (random t)

def padBits {T : ℕ} (bits : Fin T → Bool) (j : ℕ) : Bool :=
  if h : j < T then bits ⟨j, h⟩ else false

def result (M : Machine) (G : Game) (T : ℕ) (bits : Fin T → Bool) : Configuration M :=
  run M (input G) (padBits bits) T

def CorrectOutput (G : Game) {M : Machine} (c : Configuration M) : Prop :=
  c.control = none ∧
  (∀ v : Fin G.n, c.tape 2 (v.val : ℤ) = some true ↔ Winning G v) ∧
  (∀ v : Fin G.n, c.tape 2 (v.val : ℤ) = some false ↔ ¬ Winning G v) ∧
  ∀ j : ℤ, (j < 0 ∨ (G.n : ℤ) ≤ j) → c.tape 2 j = none

noncomputable def MainClaim : Prop := by
  classical
  exact ∃ M : Machine, ∃ C : ℝ, 0 < C ∧ ∀ G : Game, ∃ T : ℕ,
    (T : ℝ) ≤ (2 : ℝ) ^ (C * (Real.logb 2 ((input G).length + 2)) ^ 2) ∧
    (∀ bits : Fin T → Bool, (result M G T bits).control = none) ∧
    7 * 2 ^ T ≤ 8 * (Finset.univ.filter
      (fun bits : Fin T → Bool => CorrectOutput G (result M G T bits))).card

end MeanPayoff

theorem randomized_quasipolynomial_mean_payoff : MeanPayoff.MainClaim := by
  sorry

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We give a randomized algorithm that computes the complete zero-threshold winning set of a finite mean-payoff game with arbitrary signed integer edge weights encoded in binary. For total explicit input length <i>L</i>, it uses $`2^{O((\log(L+2))^2)}`$ bit operations on every random tape and is correct with probability at least 7/8. A polynomial-time check certifies the winning regions and positional strategies for both players or reports failure. Independent repetition therefore gives an always-correct algorithm with the same expected quasipolynomial bit bound.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

A mean-payoff game asks whether a player can maintain a nonnegative long-run average reward while an opponent chooses some of the moves. The arena is finite, but plays are infinite and integer edge weights may have large magnitudes. Positional strategies provide finite certificates for the two winning regions. The algorithmic difficulty is to find those regions with a cost controlled by the binary description, rather than by the numerical size of a weight.

This paper gives a randomized quasipolynomial-time algorithm. Its central output is an exact bounded integer potential: with probability at least $7/8$, every coordinate of that potential is correct. Thresholding it returns the complete winning set. The method also gives order guarantees in finite integer boxes with arbitrary endpoints and the allowed reference width. Each guarantee concerns a fixed comparison vector and all its coordinates at once.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Games, encoding, and the main theorem

The input consists of a finite directed graph $G=(V,E)$ on $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$, $n\ge1$, an ownership partition $V=V_{\mathrm{Max}}\mathbin{\dot\cup}V_{\mathrm{Min}}$, and signed integer weights $w:E\to\mathbb Z$. Every vertex has an outgoing edge; self-loops and parallel edges are permitted. At a vertex its owner chooses the next edge. Both players may use strategies depending on the entire finite history. For a play $\pi=(e_0,e_1,\ldots)$, define $$\operatorname{MP}_w(\pi)
 =\liminf_{T\to\infty}\frac1T\sum_{j=0}^{T-1}w(e_j).$$ We use an explicit binary encoding of the graph, ownership, and signed weights. Its total length is $L$; in particular, endpoints and ownership are charged as part of the input.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There is a uniform randomized algorithm that, for every such game, returns the entire set $$U=\{v\in V:\text{there is a Max strategy }\sigma
       \text{ such that for every Min strategy }\tau,\ 
       \operatorname{MP}_w(\pi_{v,\sigma,\tau})\ge0\}$$ correctly with probability at least $7/8$. For an absolute constant $C>0$, on every random tape its total cost is at most $$2^{C(\log_2(L+2))^2}$$ bit operations in a standard model polynomially equivalent to a uniform probabilistic Turing machine. The bound includes preprocessing, recursive calls, intermediate arithmetic, and random-bit generation.*

</div>

The probability statement concerns the whole returned set, and the time bound holds even when that output is wrong. Large weights are allowed: the resource parameter is their binary description together with the rest of the explicit input. The asserted bound is quasipolynomial; a polynomial-time bound would be stronger.

The recovered potential has a polynomial-time check. A run can therefore return either a verified winning set with positional strategies for both players, or a failure symbol. It returns the verified output with probability at least $7/8$ and never accepts an incorrect one. Corollary <a href="#cor:certified-output" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:certified-output">29</a> proves this consequence and an always-correct, or Las Vegas, version obtained by repeating until the check succeeds. The repeated version has quasipolynomial expected bit cost; each individual trial retains the bound on every tape. The strategies certify the zero-threshold regions, with Max enforcing nonnegative payoff on its region and Min enforcing strictly negative payoff on the complement.

The companion article *Deterministic quasipolynomial-time mean-payoff games* (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[OpenAI2026Deterministic](#ref-OpenAI2026Deterministic)] gives a deterministic complete winning-set algorithm with the same form of bit bound. Its Section 7 also reduces exact values and globally optimal positional strategies to polynomially many winning-set queries. The value at a start is the best worst-case mean payoff from that start; the two strategies attain these values simultaneously at all starts. Using amplified calls to the algorithm of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, that reduction returns the entire value and strategy output correctly with probability at least $7/8$, with the same form of bit bound on every random tape (OpenAI 2026, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[OpenAI2026Deterministic](#ref-OpenAI2026Deterministic)].

The present paper retains different outputs and a different method: pointwise whole-vector comparisons in boxes with arbitrary integer endpoints and allowed reference width, exact recovery of the top-box potential, and a randomized orientation recursion. The proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is independent of the deterministic algorithm.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Historical context and related algorithms

Ehrenfeucht and Mycielski proved the positional structure of mean-payoff games (Ehrenfeucht and Mycielski 1979) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[EhrenfeuchtMycielski1979](#ref-EhrenfeuchtMycielski1979)]. A long algorithmic line uses *potentials*, numbers assigned to vertices whose endpoint differences reweight edges without changing cycle sums. Gurvich, Karzanov, and Khachiyan developed this approach for cyclic games (Gurvich, Karzanov, and Khachiyan 1988) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[GurvichKarzanovKhachiyan1988](#ref-GurvichKarzanovKhachiyan1988)]; related algorithms and analyses were given by Karzanov and Lebedev (Karzanov and Lebedev 1993) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KarzanovLebedev1993](#ref-KarzanovLebedev1993)] and Pisaruk (Pisaruk 1999) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Pisaruk1999](#ref-Pisaruk1999)]. Zwick and Paterson established complexity results and pseudopolynomial algorithms for mean-payoff threshold and value problems (Zwick and Paterson 1996) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[ZwickPaterson1996](#ref-ZwickPaterson1996)]. A pseudopolynomial cost may be polynomial in the weight magnitude while exponential in its binary length, which is the distinction relevant here.

Energy games express a related finite-prefix requirement: starting with some credit, keep every cumulative total nonnegative. Resource interfaces and energy constraints provide a verification setting for this question (Chakrabarti et al. 2003; Bouyer et al. 2008) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ChakrabartiEtAl2003](#ref-ChakrabartiEtAl2003), [BouyerEtAl2008](#ref-BouyerEtAl2008)]. Brim, Chaloupka, Doyen, Gentilini, and Raskin use bounded integer progress measures and monotone lifting (Brim et al. 2011) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)]; their threshold algorithm has $O(|E|nM)$ arithmetic cost for an absolute weight bound $M$ (Brim et al. 2011, Theorem 8) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)]. Lifshits and Pavlov give a paired potential characterization and an exponential algorithm (Lifshits and Pavlov 2007) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[LifshitsPavlov2007](#ref-LifshitsPavlov2007)]. Cadilhac, Casares, and Ohlmann develop a uniform account of sound potential updates and accelerated value iteration for energy games (Cadilhac, Casares, and Ohlmann 2025) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CadilhacCasaresOhlmann2025](#ref-CadilhacCasaresOhlmann2025)]. Our box comparison method uses this potential viewpoint, with a symmetric boundary convention and a locally proved game reduction.

Strategy improvement supplies another route. For graphs without parallel edges, Björklund and Vorobyov obtain an expected $2^{O(\sqrt{n\log n})}$ arithmetic-operation bound for the threshold partition (Björklund and Vorobyov 2007, Theorem 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BjorklundVorobyov2007](#ref-BjorklundVorobyov2007)], following earlier work with Sandberg (Björklund, Sandberg, and Vorobyov 2004) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BjorklundSandbergVorobyov2004](#ref-BjorklundSandbergVorobyov2004)]. Andersson and Vorobyov use discounted linear programs for randomized strongly subexponential value and strategy algorithms (Andersson and Vorobyov 2006) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[AnderssonVorobyov2006](#ref-AnderssonVorobyov2006)]. Subsequent analyses include Zwick’s bounds for Random-Action-Removal (Zwick 2026) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Zwick2026](#ref-Zwick2026)] and the bounds of Dorfman, Kaplan, and Zwick for Switch-All and Random-Edge in energy games (Dorfman, Kaplan, and Zwick 2026) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[DorfmanKaplanZwick2026](#ref-DorfmanKaplanZwick2026)]. These comparisons require two distinctions. Arithmetic counts must be supplemented by operand bit lengths. Moreover, an expected Las Vegas bit bound can be converted to a bounded-error bound on every tape by a fixed cutoff. Thus the quantitative contribution of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> is its quasipolynomial dependence on $L$, together with the specified complete output and potential guarantees.

The 2017 quasipolynomial parity-game algorithm of Calude, Jain, Khoussainov, Li, and Stephan (Calude et al. 2022) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CaludeJainKhoussainovLiStephan2022](#ref-CaludeJainKhoussainovLiStephan2022)] opened a further direction. In a parity game, the winner is determined by vertex priorities appearing infinitely often. Extensions to quantitative objectives retain additional parameters: Daviaud, Jurdziński, and Lazić combine succinct parity progress measures with energy bounds in a pseudo-quasipolynomial algorithm for mean-payoff parity games (Daviaud, Jurdziński, and Lazić 2018) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[DaviaudJurdzinskiLazic2018](#ref-DaviaudJurdzinskiLazic2018)]. Fijalkow, Gawrychowski, and Ohlmann obtain universal-graph bounds involving numerical weight magnitude or an exponential dependence on the number of distinct weights (Fijalkow, Gawrychowski, and Ohlmann 2020) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[FijalkowGawrychowskiOhlmann2020](#ref-FijalkowGawrychowskiOhlmann2020)]. The later theory also proves mean-payoff universal-graph size lower bounds (Colcombet et al. 2022, sec. 7) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[ColcombetFijalkowGawrychowskiOhlmann2022](#ref-ColcombetFijalkowGawrychowskiOhlmann2022)]. Such lower bounds concern that representation of the objective and do not bound the complexity of every algorithm.

Randomness in the input leads to a different comparison. Loff and Skomra prove high-probability polynomial bounds for independent Gaussian perturbations of normalized payoffs on ergodic game graphs (Loff and Skomra 2024, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[LoffSkomra2024](#ref-LoffSkomra2024)]. Their bound also depends on the perturbation parameter. Here the input is arbitrary and fixed; only the algorithm is random.

A dominion in a parity game is a vertex set in which one player can keep the play and win. Parys’s recursion uses separate bounds on the sizes of the two players’ dominions. Among calls with a reduced bound, it makes one call retaining both current bounds (Parys 2019, Algorithm 2 and Section 5) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Parys2019](#ref-Parys2019)]. Recursive refinements are presented by Lehtinen, Parys, Schewe, and Wojtczak (Lehtinen et al. 2022, secs. 3–4) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[LehtinenParysScheweWojtczak2022](#ref-LehtinenParysScheweWojtczak2022)]. Our separate support budgets parallel these bounds on protected objects. Here the objects are potential vectors, and each probabilistic comparison is stated for one fixed vector; the parity-game contracts cover all qualifying dominions simultaneously.

Ohlmann’s symmetric recursion for mean-payoff games supplies a related potential viewpoint and leaves its subexponential complexity analysis open (Ohlmann 2026, secs. 3–5 and 7) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Ohlmann2026](#ref-Ohlmann2026)]. We prove the weighted support invariant, the sampled orientation argument, and the resulting bit bound below, without importing a recursive solver as an oracle.

Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman establish reductions between tropical feasibility and winning initial states in mean-payoff games (Akian, Gaubert, and Guterman 2012, Theorem 3.2 and Corollary 3.7) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[AkianGaubertGuterman2012](#ref-AkianGaubertGuterman2012)]. There are also strongly polynomial claims based on tropical optimization and max-atoms (Truffet 2025, 2026) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Truffet2025MaxAtoms](#ref-Truffet2025MaxAtoms), [Truffet2026SubstitutionV4](#ref-Truffet2026SubstitutionV4)]. Appendix <a href="#sec:tropical-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tropical-comparison">9</a> examines the printed substitution rule in Truffet’s arXiv version 4 of August 29, 2026 and gives an explicit instance on which it loses the optimum. The exact theorem and procedure text of the distinct 2025 HAL/MSR claim was unavailable to us; the appendix makes no assertion about that proof. Its objection is specific to the cited version and rule. Neither claim is an input to our proof.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### How the solver finds the potential

First transform each weight to $(n+1)w(e)+1$. This familiar integer perturbation separates the signs of simple-cycle sums (Björklund and Vorobyov 2007, sec. 9) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[BjorklundVorobyov2007](#ref-BjorklundVorobyov2007)]. Given a vector $p$, let $F_i(p)$ be the maximum, at a Max vertex, or minimum, at a Min vertex, of a transformed outgoing-edge weight plus the coordinate at its head. The operator preserves order and commutes with common scalar shifts. In a finite integer box $[a,b]=\{p\in\mathbb Z^V:a\le p\le b\}$, with coordinatewise inequalities, impose $y_i\le F_i(y)$ only where $y_i>a_i$ for a subsolution, and impose the reversed inequality only where a supersolution lies below $b_i$. Every subsolution lies below every supersolution. Clipping, or truncating each coordinate of $F$ to its interval, therefore yields a unique fixed point. In a sufficiently wide constant box its coordinates fall into two separated bands, from which positional winning strategies follow.

Ordinary monotone iteration can take time proportional to the box width. We replace that dependence by recursion on the supports of the comparisons. The support consists of the coordinates where the defining inequality is imposed. Each side receives positive rational vertex weights and its own budget. The recursive answer is constrained by its order relative to each fixed admissible comparison; the answer itself need not satisfy the comparison inequalities. Reducing a budget, or halving weights on a suitable concentrated part of a support, decreases a scalar rank parameter.

At a large box the solver first tightens its bounds, then chooses which boundary to truncate. The auxiliary procedure called orientation returns a choice of side and a list of vertex sets of small auxiliary measure. For each fixed comparison whose support is sufficiently concentrated near the threatened boundary, it either protects that side or returns a set containing more than the required share of its support weight. Reweighting on such a set makes a smaller-rank call applicable.

The orientation proof follows a sequence of centers. At each center, two smaller-rank calls reduce the two budgets separately, and the next center is their clipped average. Successful comparisons ensure that the maximum deviation of the fixed comparison never grows. Whenever its projection fits the reduced budget, that deviation decreases by a controlled amount. Many such decreases force a visible displacement of the extreme coordinates. If there are few, a newly sampled uniform iterate is likely to have a heavy projected support in a smaller box. The sampled comparison is fixed before the child’s fresh random bits, and support containment transports the child’s conclusion back.

This order of conditioning is the reason pointwise guarantees suffice. No event protecting all admissible vectors in one run is used. Amplification controls the fresh smaller-rank calls. The one continuation with unchanged support parameters is kept unamplified, and its entire chain is analyzed together, both for errors and for work. At the top box, the unique deterministic solution is an admissible comparison on both sides. Intersecting its two whole-vector success events gives exact recovery with probability at least $7/8$.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:boxes">2</a> establishes finite-box comparison and the game reduction. Section <a href="#sec:budgets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:budgets">3</a> proves the support and projection rules. Section <a href="#sec:algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:algorithm">4</a> specifies the complete recursion and proves termination on every tape. Under a lower-rank solver hypothesis, Section <a href="#sec:orientation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:orientation">5</a> proves orientation; Section <a href="#sec:solving" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:solving">6</a> then closes that rank induction and proves exact top-potential recovery. Section <a href="#sec:complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:complexity">7</a> bounds the full bit cost, and Section <a href="#sec:completion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:completion">8</a> assembles the game algorithm and derives the certifying and Las Vegas versions. Appendix <a href="#sec:tropical-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:tropical-comparison">9</a> is an independent version-specific comparison.

<a id="sec:boxes"></a>
### Integer boxes and the winning-set reduction

The game will be solved by recovering one integer potential. We first construct its operator and prove a comparison principle in an arbitrary finite box. The principle gives both a unique clipped fixed point and an exact, possibly slow, iteration. We then identify the winning set from the fixed point in a particular wide box. This order separates the reusable comparison result from the width needed for the game reduction.

Potential transformations originate in cyclic-game algorithms (Gurvich, Karzanov, and Khachiyan 1988) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[GurvichKarzanovKhachiyan1988](#ref-GurvichKarzanovKhachiyan1988)]; bounded monotone integer updates also underlie energy progress measures (Brim et al. 2011, Definitions 1–2 and Lemmas 4–6) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[BrimEtAl2011](#ref-BrimEtAl2011)]. The symmetric box statements below are proved directly.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The transformed operator

Use coordinatewise order, maxima, and minima throughout. A scalar added to a vector is added in every coordinate. For integer vectors $a\le b$, set $$[a,b]=\{p\in\mathbb Z^V:a_i\le p_i\le b_i\text{ for all }i\in V\},
 \qquad
 (\mathop{\mathrm{clip}}_{[a,b]}p)_i=\min\{b_i,\max\{a_i,p_i\}\}.$$ A positive integer $B$ is a *reference width* if $b_i-a_i\le B$ for all $i$. Endpoints may be negative or vary with $i$, and an interval may collapse to a single integer.

<a id="eq:transformed"></a>
Apply the integer cycle perturbation used by Björklund and Vorobyov (Björklund and Vorobyov 2007, sec. 9) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[BjorklundVorobyov2007](#ref-BjorklundVorobyov2007)], and define $$t(e)=(n+1)w(e)+1,\qquad
 W=\max\bigl\{1,\max_{e\in E}|t(e)|\bigr\},\qquad B_*=8nW.
 \label{eq:transformed}$$ A simple directed cycle has no repeated vertex except its two endpoints; a self-loop therefore has length one.

<a id="stmt-box-cycle-signs"></a>
<div id="box:cycle-signs" class="lemma">

**Lemma 2** (Cycle signs). *Every simple directed cycle has nonzero $t$-sum. Its $t$-sum is positive exactly when its $w$-sum is nonnegative, and negative exactly when its $w$-sum is negative.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-box-cycle-signs"><summary>Proof of stmt-box-cycle-signs</summary>

*Proof.* For a cycle of length $\ell\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and integer $w$-sum $c$, the new sum is $(n+1)c+\ell$. If $c\ge0$ this is at least one. If $c<0$, then $c\le-1$, and the new sum is at most $-(n+1)+n=-1$. The calculation concerns the actual edges of the cycle and so also covers parallel edges. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:operator"></a>
On the whole space $\mathbb Z^V$, define $$\label{eq:operator}
 F_i(p)=
 \begin{cases}
  \displaystyle\max_{e:i\to j}(t(e)+p_j),&i\in V_{\mathrm{Max}},\\[2mm]
  \displaystyle\min_{e:i\to j}(t(e)+p_j),&i\in V_{\mathrm{Min}}.
 \end{cases}$$ The graph is finite and has no sinks, so these extrema exist and are attained. Two properties will be used repeatedly: <a id="box:operator-properties"></a>$$\label{box:operator-properties}
 p\le q\ \Longrightarrow\ F(p)\le F(q),\qquad
 F(p+c)=F(p)+c\quad(c\in\mathbb Z).$$ Defining $F$ outside any particular box is essential: later comparisons will be translated before being clipped into new boxes.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Comparison and exact iteration

<a id="stmt-def-box-comparisons"></a>
<div id="def:box-comparisons" class="definition">

**Definition 3** (Box comparisons). For a finite integer box $[a,b]$, a *subsolution* is a full vector $y\in[a,b]$ satisfying $$y_i\le F_i(y)\quad(i\in S_y),\qquad S_y=\{i\in V:y_i>a_i\}.$$ A *supersolution* is a full vector $z\in[a,b]$ satisfying $$z_i\ge F_i(z)\quad(i\in S_z),\qquad S_z=\{i\in V:z_i<b_i\}.$$ We call $S_y$ and $S_z$ their *supports*, always relative to the specified box. At a collapsed coordinate $a_i=b_i$, neither support contains $i$ and neither inequality is required.

</div>

The comparison inequalities are imposed only on their respective supports. Nevertheless, their conclusion orders the vectors everywhere.

<a id="stmt-lem-box-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:box-comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Comparison principle). *Let $F$ be the transformed operator in Equation <a href="#eq:operator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:operator">[eq:operator]</a>. For arbitrary integer bounds $a\le b$, every subsolution $y\in[a,b]$ and supersolution $z\in[a,b]$ satisfy $y_i\le z_i$ for *all* $i\in V$. This includes boxes with negative, nonconstant, or collapsed coordinate intervals.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-box-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-box-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that $d=\max_i(y_i-z_i)>0$, and take a vertex attaining this maximum. There $y_i>z_i\ge a_i$ and $z_i<y_i\le b_i$, so both defining inequalities apply. Since $y\le z+d$, monotonicity and translation give $$d=y_i-z_i\le F_i(y)-F_i(z)\le d.$$ Equality forces both comparison slacks to vanish: <a id="box:comparison-equalities"></a>$$\label{box:comparison-equalities}
 y_i=F_i(y),\qquad z_i=F_i(z).$$ We claim that a common tight edge leads to another vertex attaining $d$. At a Max vertex choose an edge $e:i\to j$ attaining $F_i(y)$; at a Min vertex choose one attaining $F_i(z)$. In either case, $$d=F_i(y)-F_i(z)
